\Supp_{R_{\infty}^{\prime}}(H^{*}(C_{\infty}^{\prime}))=\Spec T_{\infty}^{\prime}
. (This is because the kernel of
T_{\infty}\to\End_{S_{\infty}}(H^{*}(C_{\infty}))
is nilpotent and the same for
T_{\infty}^{\prime}
and
C_{\infty}^{\prime}
.)

Proposition 5.4.2
.

Consider the automorphic subset of
\Spec R_{\infty}
:

\Supp_{R_{\infty}}(H^{*}(C_{\infty}))=\Spec T_{\infty}\subset\Spec R_{\infty}.

(1)

There exists an irreducible component
C_{a}\subset\Spec R_{\infty}
, containing the automorphic point
x
, with
C_{a}\subset\Spec T_{\infty}
.

(2)

Let
C_{a}\subset\Spec T_{\infty}
be an irreducible component of
\Spec R_{\infty}
which contains
x
. Suppose
C\subset\Spec R_{\infty}
is an irreducible component such that the subsets
C\cap\Spec(R_{\infty}/\varpi)
and
C_{a}\cap\Spec(R_{\infty}/\varpi)
of
\Spec(R_{\infty}/\varpi)
contain generic points
\overline{x}_{C}
,
\overline{x}_{a}
respectively, which generalize to the same generic point
x^{\prime}
of
\Spec R^{\prime}_{\infty}
. Then
C\subset\Spec T_{\infty}
.

Proof.

First we note that the pullback of
x
to
S_{\infty}
is
\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}
. We write
\tilde{x}\in\Spec(T_{\infty}\otimes_{S_{\infty}}S_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}})
for the prime ideal extending
x
.

It follows from our assumptions and
[
CG18
, Lemma 6.2]
, just as in the proof of Proposition
[
ACC+18
, Proposition 6.3.8]
, that
H^{*}(C_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}})
is non-zero exactly in degree
q_{0}+l_{0}
and that
M_{\infty}:=H^{q_{0}+l_{0}}(C_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}})
is a Cohen–Macaulay
S_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}}
-module with depth and dimension equal to
\dim S_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}}-l_{0}=\dim S_{\infty}-l_{0}-1
.

Since the image of
\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}S_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}}
in
\End(H^{q_{0}+l_{0}}(C_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}})))
is contained in the image of
\tilde{x}
, we deduce that
\mathrm{depth}(\tilde{x},M_{\infty})\geq\dim S_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}}-l_{0}
. An
M_{\infty}
-regular sequence in
\tilde{x}
remains regular on
M_{\infty,\tilde{x}}
, and the localization
(T_{\infty}\otimes_{S_{\infty}}S_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}})_{\tilde{x}}
is equal to
T_{\infty,x}
. So
\mathrm{depth}_{T_{\infty,x}}(M_{\infty,\tilde{x}})\geq\dim S_{\infty,\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}}-l_{0}
. In particular,
\dim T_{\infty,x}\geq\dim S_{\infty}-l_{0}-1
, so the one-dimensional prime
x
is contained in an irreducible component of
\Spec T_{\infty}
of dimension at least
\dim S_{\infty}-l_{0}
. By dimension considerations, this irreducible component can be identified with an irreducible component of
\Spec R_{\infty}
. This shows the existence of an irreducible component
C_{a}
as in the first part.

For the second part, we let
x_{a}
be the generic point of
C_{a}
. Since the pullback of
x_{a}
to
S_{\infty}
is contained in
\mathfrak{a}_{\infty}
, the ‘localization’
C_{\infty,x_{a}}
defined following
[
ACC+18
, Lemma 6.3.3]
is quasi-isomorphic to the complex with
M_{\infty,x_{a}}
in degree
q_{0}+l_{0}
and zero elsewhere. In particular, with the length function on complexes defined in
[
ACC+18
, §6.3.1]
, we have
\lg_{T_{\infty,x_{a}}}(C_{\infty,x_{a}})\neq 0
. The generic point
\overline{x}_{a}
of
\Spec R_{\infty}/(x_{a},\varpi)
given to us in the statement of the proposition has dimension
\dim S_{\infty}-l_{0}-1
and is a generic point of
\Spec R_{\infty}/(\varpi)
which lies in
\Spec T_{\infty}
. Let
\overline{x}_{a}^{\prime}
denote the corresponding point of
\Spec R_{\infty}^{\prime}/(\varpi)
. It has a unique generalization
x^{\prime}\in\Spec R_{\infty}^{\prime}
.

Now let
x_{C}
be the generic point of
C
. We wish to show that it lies in
\Spec T_{\infty}
. We are given a generic point
\overline{x}_{C}
of
\Spec R_{\infty}/(x_{C},\varpi)
, which must have dimension
\dim S_{\infty}-l_{0}-1
and be a generic point of
\Spec R_{\infty}/(\varpi)
. Let
\overline{x}_{C}^{\prime}
denote the corresponding point of
\Spec R_{\infty}^{\prime}/(\varpi)
, which also generalizes to
x^{\prime}
by hypothesis.

We now repeatedly use
[
ACC+18
, Lemma 6.3.7]
.
As
\lg_{T_{\infty,x_{a}}}(C_{\infty,x_{a}})\neq(0)
, we deduce that
\lg_{T_{\infty,\overline{x}_{a}}}((C_{\infty}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{S_{\infty}}S_{\infty}/(\varpi))_{\overline{x}_{a}})\neq 0
. Hence
\overline{x}_{a}^{\prime}\in\Spec T_{\infty}^{\prime}
and
\lg_{T_{\infty,\overline{x}_{a}^{\prime}}^{\prime}}((C_{\infty}^{\prime}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{S_{\infty}}S_{\infty}/(\varpi))_{\overline{x}_{a}^{\prime}})\neq 0
, from which we deduce that
x^{\prime}\in\Spec T_{\infty}^{\prime}
and
\lg_{T_{\infty,x^{\prime}}^{\prime}}(C^{\prime}_{\infty,x^{\prime}})\neq 0
. We further deduce that
\overline{x}_{C}^{\prime}\in\Spec T_{\infty}^{\prime}
and
\lg_{T_{\infty,\overline{x}_{C}^{\prime}}^{\prime}}((C_{\infty}^{\prime}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{S_{\infty}}S_{\infty}/(\varpi))_{\overline{x}_{C}^{\prime}})\neq 0
. Hence
\overline{x}_{C}\in\Spec T_{\infty}
and
\lg_{T_{\infty,\overline{x}_{C}}}((C_{\infty}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{S_{\infty}}S_{\infty}/(\varpi))_{\overline{x}_{C}})\neq 0
, from which we finally deduce that
x_{C}\in\Spec T_{\infty}
(and
\lg_{T_{\infty,x_{C}}}(C_{\infty,x_{C}})\neq(0)
).
∎

Corollary 5.4.3
.

Let
C
be an irreducible component of
\Spec R_{\infty}
satisfying the assumption of Proposition
5.4.2
(
2
) for some ‘automorphic’ component
C_{a}
. Let
x
be a point of
C
, and let
y
be the contraction of
x
in

S_{\infty}
. Then the support of
H^{\ast}(C_{\infty}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{S_{\infty}}S_{\infty}/y)_{y}
over
\Spec R_{\infty}
contains

x
. If
y
is one-dimensional of characteristic
0
this says that
x
is in the support of
H^{\ast}(C_{\infty}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{S_{\infty}}S_{\infty}/y)[1/p]
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
5.4.2
(
2
) by considering the
\Tor
spectral sequence computing the cohomology of
C_{\infty,y}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{S_{\infty,y}}S_{\infty,y}/y
, as in the proof of
[
ACC+18
, Corollary 6.3.9]
. ∎

5.5.
Hecke algebras and cohomology of locally symmetric spaces for
\PGL_{2}

We now go back to the constructions of §
2.1.2
, which we apply to
\mathrm{G}=\overline{G}=\PGL_{2,F}
for an imaginary CM field
F
. We need to drop the assumption that
K=\prod_{v}K_{v}\subset\overline{G}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,f})
is neat. We assume for convenience that
K\subset\PGL_{2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F})
. Thanks to the results of
[
AKT23
, §5]
, all the properties we need for cohomology of locally symmetric spaces for
\PGL_{2}
can be deduced from the case of
\GL_{2}
with neat level.

We fix a finite set
S
of finite places of
F
such that
S_{p}\subset S
and
K_{v}=\PGL_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{v}})
for
v\notin S
. We assume that
R={\mathcal{O}}
or
{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}
for some
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
. Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a
R[K_{S}]
-module, finite free as an
R
-module, and such that
{\mathcal{V}}/\varpi^{r}
is a smooth
K_{S}
-module for each
r\geq 1
.

We will make use of the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}(\overline{G}^{S},K^{S})
. For each finite place
v\notin S
and
1\leq i\leq 2
, we write
T_{v,i}
for the image of
T_{v,i}\in{\mathcal{H}}(\GL_{2}(F_{v}),\GL_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{v}}))
in
{\mathcal{H}}(\PGL_{2}(F_{v}),\PGL_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{v}}))
. In fact
T_{v,2}=1
in
{\mathcal{H}}(\overline{G}^{S},K^{S})
. We write
P_{v}(X)
for the image of the polynomial (
2.1.5
) in
{\mathcal{H}}(\PGL_{2}(F_{v}),\PGL_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{v}}))[X]
.

We consider the object

C^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{V}}):=\varprojlim_{r}R\Gamma(K,R\Gamma(\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\overline{G}},{\mathcal{V}}/\varpi^{r}))

of
D^{+}(R)
(we do not need to take a limit if
R
is finite), which comes equipped with an action of
{\mathcal{H}}(\overline{G}^{S},K^{S})
.

More generally, if
K^{\prime}=\prod K^{\prime}_{v}\subset K
is an open normal subgroup with
(K^{\prime})^{S}=K^{S}
, then we consider the object

C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})=\varprojlim_{r}R\Gamma(K^{\prime},R\Gamma(\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\overline{G}},{\mathcal{V}}/\varpi^{r}))

of
D^{+}(R[K/K^{\prime}])
, which again comes with an action of
{\mathcal{H}}(\overline{G}^{S},K^{S})
.

We can construct
C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})
, with its Hecke action, by taking a derived limit of the complexes
\Hom_{{\mathbb{Z}}[K^{\prime}]}(C_{\bullet},{\mathcal{V}}/\varpi^{r})
, where
C_{\bullet}
denotes the complex of singular chains with
{\mathbb{Z}}
-coefficients on
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\overline{G}}
. Commuting the derived limit with cohomology of
K/K^{\prime}
, using
[
Sta13
,
Tag 08U1
]
, we see that
R\Gamma(K/K^{\prime},C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}}))=C^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{V}})
.

Lemma 5.5.1
.

There are natural Hecke equivariant quasi-isomorphisms
A(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})\cong C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})
, where
A(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})
are the complexes constructed in
[
AKT23
, §5.1]
using the singular chains of
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\overline{G}}^{\mathrm{dis}}
.

Proof.

We can reduce to the case where
{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}/\varpi^{r}
and
K^{\prime}
is neat. Pullback by the continuous map
\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\overline{G}}^{\mathrm{dis}}\to\overline{\mathfrak{X}}_{\overline{G}}
induces a Hecke equivariant map
A(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})\to C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})
inducing the identity on the cohomology groups, which are identified with
H^{*}(\overline{X}^{\overline{G}}_{K^{\prime}},{\mathcal{V}})
on both sides.
∎

Since
K
is not assumed to be neat,
C^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{V}})
is not necessarily a perfect complex. However, using the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence to compute its cohomology in terms of
C^{\bullet}(K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})
for
K^{\prime}\subset K
a neat open normal subgroup, we see that its cohomology groups are finitely generated
R
-modules
[
AKT23
, Lemma 5.1]
.

A similar argument shows that the
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras

{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{\overline{G}}(C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}}))\subset\End_{D^{+}(R[K/K^{\prime}])}(C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}}))

generated by the image of
{\mathcal{H}}(\overline{G}^{S},K^{S})
are
{\mathcal{O}}
-finite
[
AKT23
, Lemma 5.2]
.

As a consequence, for a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{\overline{G}}(C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}}))
, we have a direct summand
C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})_{\mathfrak{m}}
cut out by an idempotent
e_{\mathfrak{m}}
in the Hecke algebra
[
AKT23
, Prop. 3.6]

Proposition 5.5.2
.

Suppose that
p
is odd, and that
S
satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

S
is stable under complex conjugation

(2)

F
contains an imaginary quadratic field. Let
v\notin S
be a finite place, with residue characteristic
l
. Then either
S
contains no
l
-adic places and
l
is unramified in
F
, or there exists an imaginary quadratic subfield of
F
in which
l
splits.

Then for any maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{\overline{G}}(C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}}))
, there exists a semisimple continuous representation

\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{F,S}\to\GL_{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{\overline{G}}(C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}}))/\mathfrak{m})

such that for each
v\notin S
,

\det(X-\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathrm{Frob}_{v}))=P_{v}(X)\mod\mathfrak{m}.

Suppose moreover that
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is absolutely irreducible. Then there exists an integer
N\geq 1
, depending only on
[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]
, an ideal
J\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{\overline{G}}(C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}}))
with
J^{N}=0
, and a continuous representation

\rho_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{F,S}\to\GL_{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{\overline{G}}(C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}}))/J)

such that for each
v\notin S
,

\det(X-\rho_{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathrm{Frob}_{v}))=P_{v}(X)\mod J.

In particular, we have
\det\rho_{\mathfrak{m}}=\epsilon_{p}^{-1}
.

Proof.

This is essentially
[
AKT23
, Cor. 5.7]
(the assumption there that
K/K^{\prime}
is abelian is not necessary). We let
K_{G}
and
K^{\prime}_{G}
be the pre-images in
\GL_{2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F})
of
K
and
K^{\prime}
respectively. Then
[
AKT23
, Corollary 5.5]
identifies
{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{\overline{G}}(H^{\ast}(C^{\bullet}(K/K^{\prime},{\mathcal{V}})))
as a quotient of a
\GL_{2}
-Hecke algebra
{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{G}(H^{\ast}(C^{\bullet}(K_{G}/K^{\prime}_{G},{\mathcal{V}})))
. We then reduce to the case where
K_{G}=K^{\prime}_{G}
is a neat level subgroup in
\GL_{2}
, as in the proofs of
[
AKT23
, Theorems 5.6, 5.8]
, and finally appeal to
[
ACC+18
, Theorems 2.3.5, 2.3.7]
. The determinant of
\rho_{\mathfrak{m}}
is
\epsilon_{p}^{-1}
by Chebotarev density, considering the constant terms of the polynomials
P_{v}(X)
.
∎

Proposition 5.5.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a maximal ideal of
{\mathbb{T}}^{S}_{\overline{G}}(C^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{V}}))
with residue field
k
. We make the following assumptions:

(1)

{\mathcal{V}}\otimes k\cong k
(with trivial action of
K_{S}
).

(2)

p
is odd and
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is absolutely irreducible.

(3)

\zeta_{p}\in F
.

Then the cohomology groups
H^{i}(C^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{V}}))_{\mathfrak{m}}
vanish for
i>\dim_{{\mathbb{R}}}X^{\overline{G}}
. In particular,
C^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{V}})_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a perfect complex of
R
-modules.

Proof.

This follows from
[
AKT23
, Thm. 5.11]
.
∎

5.6.
The proof of Theorem
5.2

We first prove a version of Theorem
5.2
with some additional assumptions. The general case will follow using solvable base change, as in
[
ACC+18
, §6.5.12]
and the proof of
[
AKT23
, Theorem A.14]
.

We fix the following data:

(1)

An imaginary CM field
F
, an odd prime
p
and an isomorphism
\iota:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\cong{\mathbb{C}}
.

(2)

A finite set
S
of finite places of
F
, including the places above
p
.

(3)

A (possibly empty) subset
R\subset S
of places prime to
p
and a decomposition
S_{p}=S_{p}^{cr}\coprod S_{p}^{st}
.

(4)

A cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})
which is regular algebraic of weight
0
. We may identify
\pi
with a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\GL_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})
with trivial central character.

We assume the following conditions are satisfied:

(5)

If
l
is a prime lying below an element of
S
, or which is ramified in
F
, then
F
contains an imaginary quadratic field in which
l
splits. In particular, each place of
S
is split over
F^{+}
and the extension
F/F^{+}
is everywhere unramified.

(6)

For each
v\in S_{p}
, let
\overline{v}
denote the place of
F^{+}
lying below
v
. Then there exists a place
\overline{v}^{\prime}\neq\overline{v}
of
F^{+}
such that
\overline{v}^{\prime}|p
and

\sum_{\overline{v}^{\prime\prime}\neq\overline{v},\overline{v}^{\prime}}[F^{+}_{\overline{v}^{\prime\prime}}:\mathbf{Q}_{p}]>\frac{1}{2}[F^{+}:\mathbf{Q}].

Moreover, we assume that the residue field of
v
is strictly bigger than
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
.

(7)

\pi_{v}
is unramified for
v\notin R\cup S_{p}^{st}
.

(8)

If
v\in R\cup S_{p}^{st}
, then
\pi_{v}^{\mathrm{Iw}_{v}}\neq 0
.

(9)

If
v\in S_{p}^{st}
, then
\pi
is
\iota
-ordinary of weight
0
at
v
and
r_{\iota}(\pi)|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is non-crystalline ordinary.

(10)

If
S=S_{p}\cup R
, then
\zeta_{p}\in F
.

(11)

If
S\neq S_{p}\cup R
, then
S-(S_{p}\cup R)
contains at least two places with distinct residue characteristics.

(12)

If
v\in S-(R\cup S_{p})
, then
v\notin R^{c}
and
H^{2}(F_{v},\mathrm{ad}^{0}\overline{r}_{\pi,\iota})=0
.

(13)

\overline{r}_{\pi,\iota}
is decomposed generic and
{\overline{r}_{\pi,\iota}}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
is irreducible.

(14)

\overline{r}_{\pi,\iota}|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is the trivial representation for
v\in S_{p}\cup R
. (In particular, by considering determinants,
q_{v}\equiv 1\mod p
for
v\in R
.)

(15)

If
p=5
and the projective image of
\overline{r}_{\pi,\iota}(G_{F(\zeta_{5})})
is conjugate to
\PSL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})
, we assume further that the extension of
F
cut out by the projective image of
\overline{r}_{\pi,\iota}
does not contain
\zeta_{5}
.

Proposition 5.6.1
.

With notation and assumptions as in (
1
)–(
15
), suppose given a continuous representation
\rho:G_{F}\to\GL_{2}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

We have
\overline{\rho}\cong\overline{r}_{\pi,\iota}
and
\det(\rho)=\epsilon_{p}^{-1}
.

(2)

For each place
v\in S_{p}^{cr}
,
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is Barsotti–Tate.

(3)

For each place
v\in S_{p}^{cr}
,
r_{\iota}(\pi)|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is ordinary if and only if
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is ordinary.

(4)

For each place
v\in S_{p}^{st}
,
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is a non-crystalline extension of
\epsilon_{p}^{-1}
by the trivial character.

(5)

For each finite place
v\notin S
of
F
,
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is unramified.

(6)

For each place
v\in R
,
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is unipotently ramified.

Then
\rho
is automorphic: there exists a cuspidal automorphic representation
\Pi
of
\PGL_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})
of weight
0
such that
\rho\cong r_{\iota}(\Pi)
.

Proof.

We define a compact open subgroup
K=\prod_{v}K_{v}
of
\PGL_{2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F})
as follows:

•

If
v\notin S
or
v\in S_{p}^{cr}
, then
K_{v}=\PGL_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{v}})
.

•

If
v\in R\cup S_{p}^{st}
, then
K_{v}=\mathrm{Iw}_{v}
.

•

If
v\in S-(S_{p}\cup R)
, then
K_{v}=\mathrm{Iw}_{v,1}
is the pro-
v
Iwahori subgroup of
\PGL_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F_{v}})
.

If
S-(S_{p}\cup R)
non-empty,
K
is neat, by the same argument as
[
ACC+18
, Lemma 6.5.2]
. We set
T=S\cup S^{c}
.

Recalling the unitary group
\widetilde{G}
from §
2.1.11
(for
n=2
), we need to define standard parabolic subgroups
Q_{\bar{v}}\subset\widetilde{G}_{F^{+}_{\bar{v}}}
for each
\bar{v}\in\overline{S}_{p}
. For each
\bar{v}
we choose a place of
F
,
\tilde{v}|\bar{v}
. We make this choice so that if at least one
v|\bar{v}
is in
S_{p}^{st}
, then
\tilde{v}
is in
S_{p}^{st}
. Then for each
\bar{v}\in\overline{S}_{p}
we consider the following three cases:

(cr-ord)

\tilde{v}\in S_{p}^{st}
and
\tilde{v}^{c}\in S_{p}^{cr}
. Then
\iota_{\tilde{v}}(Q_{\bar{v}})
is the standard parabolic given by the partition
(2,1,1)
.

(ord)

\tilde{v}\in S_{p}^{st}
and
\tilde{v}^{c}\in S_{p}^{st}
. Then
\iota_{\tilde{v}}(Q_{\bar{v}})=\mathrm{B}_{4}
, the Borel subgroup.

(cr)

\tilde{v}\in S_{p}^{cr}
and
\tilde{v}^{c}\in S_{p}^{cr}
. Then
Q_{\bar{v}}=P_{\bar{v}}
, the Siegel parabolic.

We have a Hecke algebra
{\mathbb{T}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}))
, defined as in §
4.2.1
by adding Hecke operators at places
v|p
to
{\mathbb{T}}^{T}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}))
.

Then we can find a coefficient field
E\subset\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
and a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}))
such that
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{F,T}\to\GL_{n}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p})
satisfies
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}\cong\overline{r}_{\iota}(\pi)
(cf.
[
AKT23
, Theorem 5.10]
).

Moreover, for
v\in S_{p}^{st}
, since
\pi
is
\iota
-ordinary at
v
, the Hecke operators
U_{v}:=[K_{v}\iota_{v}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\varpi_{v}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}K_{v}]
are not in
\mathfrak{m}
.

Enlarging
E
if necessary, we assume that the residue field of
\mathfrak{m}
is equal to
k
and that
k
contains all eigenvalues of the elements of
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}(G_{F})
.

We now describe the global deformation problems we will be working with. They will depend on a choice of character
\chi=\prod_{v\in R}\chi_{v}:\prod_{v\in R}{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{v}}^{\times}\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
which is trivial modulo
\varpi
.

For each
\chi
, we have the global deformation problem with fixed determinant

{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}=(\overline{\rho},\epsilon_{p}^{-1},S,\{R_{v}^{\epsilon_{p}^{-1},\mathrm{BT}}\}_{v\in S_{p}^{cr}}\cup\{R_{v}^{\triangle}\}_{v\in S_{p}^{st}}\cup\{R_{v}^{\epsilon_{p}^{-1},\chi_{v}}\}_{v\in R}\cup\{R_{v}^{\epsilon_{p}^{-1}}\}_{v\in S-(S_{p}\cup R)}).

The character
\chi_{v}:{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{v}}^{\times}\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
(which we note factors through
k_{v}^{\times}
) determines a character

\displaystyle\chi_{v}:\mathrm{Iw}_{v}

\displaystyle\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mapsto\chi_{v}(a/d).

We have an
{\mathcal{O}}[K_{S}]
-module
{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1})
, where
K_{S}
acts by the projection
K_{S}\to K_{R}=\prod_{v\in R}\mathrm{Iw}_{v}\xrightarrow{\prod_{v}\chi_{v}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
.

For each
\chi
, there is a canonical, surjective,
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra map

{\mathbb{T}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1})))\to{\mathbb{T}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,k))

inducing a bijection on maximal ideals. So the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
corresponds to a maximal ideal of
{\mathbb{T}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1})))
for each
\chi
. We abusively denote all these ideals by
\mathfrak{m}
. The localisation
{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a perfect complex of
{\mathcal{O}}
-modules (using Proposition
5.5.3
when
K
is not neat). We will also consider the Hecke algebra
{\mathbb{T}}^{S,{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{m}})
obtained by adding in the spherical Hecke operators at places in
S^{c}-S
to
{\mathbb{T}}^{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{m}})
.

Proposition 5.6.2
.

There exists an integer
N\geq 1
, depending only on
[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]
, an ideal
J\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{S,{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{m}})
such that
J^{N}=0
, and a continuous surjective homomorphism

f_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}}:R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{S,{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{m}})/J

such that for each finite place
v\notin S
of
F
, the characteristic polynomial of
f_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}}\circ\rho_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}}^{\mathrm{univ}}
equals the image of
P_{v}(X)
in
{\mathbb{T}}^{S,{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{m}})/J
.

Proof.

Proposition
5.5.2
already gives us a representation of
G_{F,S\cup S^{c}}
with the right local properties at
v\notin S\cup S^{c}
, so it remains to check each prime
v\in S\cup S^{c}
. As in the proof of Proposition
5.5.2
, we reduce to proving a similar local-global compatibility statement for a neat level in
\GL_{2}
. For
v\in S_{p}
, we apply Theorem
4.2.15
. For the remaining
v
, we proceed as in the proof of
[
ACC+18
, Proposition 6.5.3]
, using
[
ACC+18
, Theorem 3.1.1]
.
∎

To complete the proof of Proposition
5.6.1
we need to show that the point of
\Spec(R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{1}})
given by
\rho
is in the support of
H^{*}(C^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}})_{\mathfrak{m}})
. Then
[
AKT23
, Theorem 5.10]
implies that
\rho
is automorphic of weight
0
.

We need a local–global compatibility statement allowing ramification at Taylor–Wiles primes. So we suppose we have a Taylor–Wiles datum
(Q,N,(\alpha_{v,1},\alpha_{v,2})_{v\in Q})
for
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
(which is then also a Taylor–Wiles datum for every
{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}
). We assume that each place of
Q
has residue characteristic split in an imaginary quadratic subfield of
F
. Now we define deformation problems

{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi,Q}=(\overline{\rho},\epsilon_{p}^{-1},S\cup Q,\{R_{v}^{\epsilon_{p}^{-1},\mathrm{BT}}\}_{v\in S_{p}^{cr}}\cup\{R_{v}^{\triangle}\}_{v\in S_{p}^{st}}\cup\{R_{v}^{\epsilon_{p}^{-1},\chi_{v}}\}_{v\in R}\cup\{R_{v}^{\epsilon_{p}^{-1}}\}_{v\in S\cup Q-(S_{p}\cup R)}).

For
v\in Q
, let
\Delta_{v}=k_{v}^{\times}(p)
, the maximal
p
-power quotient of
k_{v}^{\times}
. As in
[
AKT23
, §A.1.4]
, the local lifting ring
R_{v}^{\epsilon_{p}^{-1}}
is equipped with the structure of an
{\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{v}]
-algebra. Setting
\Delta_{Q}=\prod_{v\in Q}\Delta_{v}
, we obtain an
{\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}]
-algebra structure on
R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi,Q}}
.

We define subgroups
K_{1}(Q)\subset K_{0}(Q)\subset K
, with
K_{0}(Q)/K_{1}(Q)\cong\Delta_{Q}
as in
[
ACC+18
, §6.5]
(taking the image in
\PGL_{2}
of the subgroups defined there). From this point, we follow loc. cit. very closely, so we just explain the key points of the argument.

There is a direct summand
C^{\bullet}(K_{0}(Q)/K_{1}(Q),{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{Q}}
of
C^{\bullet}(K_{0}(Q)/K_{1}(Q),{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))
in
\mathbf{D}({\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}])
, defined using a maximal ideal in a Hecke algebra with operators
U_{v,i}
at places
v\in Q
. It is a perfect complex, by
[
AKT23
, Theorem 5.11]
.

We write
{\mathbb{T}}_{\chi,Q}
for the image of the map

{\mathbb{T}}^{S\cup Q,{\mathcal{Q}}_{\bar{S}_{p}},\bar{S}_{p}-\ord}_{\overline{G}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}]\to\End_{\mathbf{D}({\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}])}\left(C^{\bullet}(K_{0}(Q)/K_{1}(Q),{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{Q}}\right).

Proposition 5.6.3
.

There exists an integer
N\geq 1
, depending only on
[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]
, an ideal
J\subset{\mathbb{T}}_{\chi,Q}
such that
J^{N}=0
, and a continuous surjective
{\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}]
-algebra homomorphism

f_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi,Q}}:R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi,Q}}\to{\mathbb{T}}_{\chi,Q}/J

such that for each finite place
v\notin S\cup Q
of
F
, the characteristic polynomial of
f_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi,Q}}\circ\rho_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi,Q}}^{\mathrm{univ}}
equals the image of
P_{v}(X)
in
{\mathbb{T}}_{\chi,Q}/J
.

Proof.

This is proved in the same way as Proposition
5.6.2
, using
[
ACC+18
, Theorem 3.1.1]
to show that
f_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi,Q}}
is an
{\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}]
-algebra homomorphism (cf.
[
ACC+18
, Proposition 6.5.11]
and
[
AKT23
, Proposition A.13]
).
∎

It is convenient to patch complexes computing homology, so we define

C_{\chi,Q}:=R\Hom_{{\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}]}(C^{\bullet}(K_{0}(Q)/K_{1}(Q),{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{Q}},{\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}])

and

C_{\chi}:=R\Hom_{{\mathcal{O}}}(C^{\bullet}(K,{\mathcal{O}}(\chi^{-1}))_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{O}}).

Lemma 5.6.4
.

C_{\chi,Q}
is a perfect complex of
{\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}]
-modules, with a canonical isomorphism

C_{\chi,Q}\otimes^{{\mathbb{L}}}_{{\mathcal{O}}[\Delta_{Q}]}{\mathcal{O}}\cong C_{\chi}

in
\mathbf{D}({\mathcal{O}})
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that we can identify
C_{\chi,Q}
and
C_{\chi}
with the duals of perfect complexes computing localisations (at
\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{Q}
and
\mathfrak{m}
respectively) of equivariant homology (see the second part of
[
AKT23
, Theorem 5.11]
).
∎

We now have everything we need to construct the objects required for §
5.4
, using
[
ACC+18
, §6.4]
and
[
AKT23
, Proposition A.6]
(existence of Taylor–Wiles primes) as in the proof of
[
AKT23
, Theorem A.7]
. In particular, we make use of two options for the tuple of characters
\chi
: firstly,
\chi=1
, and secondly a fixed tuple, denoted
\chi
, given by a choice of character
\chi_{v}:{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{v}}^{\times}\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
with
\chi_{v}^{2}\neq 1
which is trivial mod
\varpi
for each
v\in R
.

The rings
R_{\infty}
and
R^{\prime}_{\infty}
are power series rings over
R_{\mathrm{loc}}=R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}^{S,\mathrm{loc}}
and
R^{\prime}_{\mathrm{loc}}=R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}}^{S,\mathrm{loc}}
respectively, which come equipped with local
R_{\mathrm{loc}}
-algebra (respectively
R^{\prime}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-algebra) surjections
R_{\infty}\to R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}
(respectively
R^{\prime}_{\infty}\to R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}}
). We can assume, extending
{\mathcal{O}}
if necessary, that all of the irreducible components of the local lifting rings appearing in the deformation problems
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}
(and their special fibres) are geometrically irreducible. We do this so that we can apply
[
BLGHT11
, Lemma 3.3]
.

It follows from formal smoothness of
R_{v}^{\square,\epsilon_{p}^{-1}}
for
v\in S-(R\cup S_{p})
,
[
AKT23
, Lemma A.2]
, Proposition
5.3.2
, Lemma
5.3.3
and
[
BLGHT11
, Lemma 3.3]
(which describes irreducible components of completed tensor products in terms of their factors) that
R_{\infty}
and
R^{\prime}_{\infty}
satisfy Assumption
5.4.1
. In particular,
\Spec(R^{\prime}_{\infty})
has
2^{|S_{p}|}
irreducible components, which biject with the irreducible components of
R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\chi}}^{S_{p},\mathrm{loc}}=R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}^{S_{p},\mathrm{loc}}
. We let
C_{a}
be an irreducible component of
\Spec(R_{\infty})
containing the point
r_{\pi,\iota}
with
C_{a}\subset\Spec(T_{\infty})
. It exists by Proposition
5.4.2
. We let
C
be an irreducible component of
\Spec(R_{\infty})
containing the point given by
\rho
. It follows from conditions (
3
) and (
4
), Proposition
5.3.2
, Lemma
5.3.4
and
[
BLGHT11
, Lemma 3.3]
that the generic points of
C\cap\Spec(R_{\infty}/\varpi)
and
C_{a}\cap\Spec(R_{\infty}/\varpi)
lie in the same irreducible component of
\Spec(R^{\prime}_{\infty})
. Note that condition (
4
) ensure that both
C
and
C_{a}
necessarily lie over the component
X^{st}
in
\Spec(R_{v}^{\triangle})
for
v\in S_{p}^{st}
.

We will therefore be able to apply Corollary
5.4.3
to deduce automorphy of
\rho
.

This completes the proof of Proposition
5.6.1
.
∎

The end of the proof of Theorem
5.2
.

We will use a variant of
[
DDT97
, Lemma 4.11]
:

Lemma 5.6.5
.

Suppose that
G
is a finite group with a representatation
\overline{\rho}:G\to\GL_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
for an odd prime
p
. Suppose that the character
\det\overline{\rho}:G\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
has order
d>1
, and for all
g
with
\det\overline{\rho}(g)\neq 1
we have

(5.6.1)

(\mathrm{tr}\overline{\rho}(g))^{2}=(1+\det\overline{\rho}(g))^{2}.

Then
\overline{\rho}|_{\ker(\det\overline{\rho})}
is reducible.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
[
DDT97
, Lemma 4.11]
, except when
d=3
. So we assume
d=3
. We write
Z\subset G
for the subgroup
Z=\{g\in G:\overline{\rho}(g)\text{ scalar}\}
. As in loc. cit., (
5.6.1
) implies that
Z\subset\ker\det\overline{\rho}
, so
\det\overline{\rho}
induces a surjective homomorphism
G^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow C_{d}
, where
G^{\prime}
is the projective image of
\overline{\rho}
. Dickson’s classification implies that
\overline{\rho}
is reducible or
G^{\prime}\cong A_{4}
. In the latter case, set
G_{1}=\ker(\det\overline{\rho})
. We have
\overline{\rho}(G_{1})/\overline{\rho}(Z)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2
, and it follows that
\overline{\rho}|_{\ker(\det\overline{\rho})}
is reducible.
∎

It suffices to prove that
\rho\cong r_{\iota}(\Pi)
for a cuspidal automorphic representation
\Pi
of
\GL_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
; then the fact that
\det(\rho)=\epsilon_{p}^{-1}
implies that
\Pi
has trivial central character. Let
L/F(\zeta_{p})
be the extension cut out by
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
. If
F^{\prime}/F
is any finite solvable extension, we denote the base change of
\pi
to
F^{\prime}
by
\pi_{F^{\prime}}
.

We choose a finite set
V
of finite places of
F
exactly as in the proof of
[
AKT23
, Theorem A.14]
, so that:

•

For any proper extension
L^{\prime}/F
contained in
L
, there is some
v\in V
not splitting in
L^{\prime}
.

•

There is a rational prime
q\neq p
such that
\overline{\rho}
is decomposed generic for
q
and
V
contains all
q
-adic places of
K
.

•

For each
v\in V
,
v\nmid 2p
and both
\rho
and
\pi
are unramified at
v
.

This ensures that if
F^{\prime}/F
is a finite Galois extension in which every place of
V
splits, then
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F^{\prime}}}
remains decomposed generic and
\overline{\rho}(G_{F^{\prime}(\zeta_{p})})=\overline{\rho}(G_{F(\zeta_{p})})
.

Now we choose a solvable, Galois, CM extension
F_{0}/F
such that:

•

Every place of
V
splits in
F_{0}
.

•

For every finite place
w
of
F_{0}
,
\pi_{F_{0}}^{\mathrm{Iw}_{w}}\neq 0
.

•

For every finite place
w\nmid p
of
F_{0}
, either both
\pi_{F_{0,w}}
and
\rho|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
are unramified, or
\rho|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is unipotently ramified,
q_{w}\equiv 1
mod
p
, and
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F_{0},w}}
is trivial.

•

For each
\bar{w}|p
in
F_{0}^{+}
,
\bar{w}
splits in
F_{0}
,
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is trivial for
w|\bar{w}
, the residue field
k_{w}
is strictly bigger than
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
and there exists a place
\overline{w}^{\prime}\neq\overline{w}
of
F^{+}_{0}
such that
\overline{w}^{\prime}|p
and

\sum_{\overline{w}^{\prime\prime}\neq\overline{w},\overline{w}^{\prime}}[F^{+}_{0,\overline{w}^{\prime\prime}}:\mathbf{Q}_{p}]>\frac{1}{2}[F^{+}_{0}:\mathbf{Q}].

•

If
w
lies over a place
v
of
F
with
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
potentially crystalline, then
\rho|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is crystalline and
\pi_{F_{0,w}}
is unramified. Moreover,
r_{\iota}(\pi)|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is crystalline and it is ordinary if and only if
\rho|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is ordinary.

•

If
w
lies over a place
v
of
F
with
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}}}
not potentially crystalline,
\rho|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is a non-crystalline extension of
\epsilon_{p}^{-1}
by the trivial character.

With respect to the penultimate item, we note that it follows from Theorem
4.3.1
that when
\pi_{F_{0,w}}
is unramified,
r_{\iota}(\pi)|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is automatically crystalline with all labelled Hodge–Tate weights equal to
(0,1)
.

Making a further solvable extension
F_{1}/F_{0}
by taking a composite with three imaginary quadratic fields, as in the proof of
[
AKT23
, Theorem A.14]
, we furthermore satisfy:

•

Let
R
be the set of finite places
w\nmid p
of
F_{1}
such that
\pi_{F_{1},w}
or
\rho|_{G_{F_{1,w}}}
are ramified. Let
S_{p}
denote the
p
-adic places of
F_{1}
and set
S^{\prime}=S_{p}\cup R
. If
l
is a rational prime lying below an element of
S^{\prime}
, or which is ramified in
F_{1}
, then
F_{1}
contains an imaginary quadratic field in which
l
splits.

We can now describe the data we need to apply Proposition
5.6.1
with
F=F_{1}
,
\rho=\rho|_{G_{F_{1}}}
and
\pi=\pi_{F_{1}}
. We have already defined the set of places
R
. We let
S_{p}^{cr}
be the set of places
w|p
where
\rho|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is crystalline and let
S_{p}^{st}
be the set of places
w|p
where
\rho|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is non-crystalline. For
w\in S_{p}^{st}
, we know (by assumption) that
r_{\iota}(\pi)|_{G_{F_{0,w}}}
is not crystalline, whilst it follows from Theorem
4.2.15
that it is ordinary.

If
\zeta_{p}\in F
, we set
S=S^{\prime}=S_{p}\cup R
. If
\zeta_{p}\notin F
(which entails
\zeta_{p}\notin F_{1}
), Lemma
5.6.5
shows that we can find an element
g\in\overline{\rho}(G_{F_{1}})
such that
\det(g)\neq 1
, and the ratio of the eigenvalues of
g
does not equal
\det(g)^{\pm 1}
. Using Chebotarev density, we can find infinitely many finite places
v_{0}
of
F_{1}
of degree
1
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
such that
v_{0}\notin S^{\prime}\cup R^{c}
,
q_{v_{0}}\not\equiv 1
mod
p
, and the ratio of the eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}(\mathrm{Frob}_{v_{0}})
does not equal
q_{v_{0}}^{\pm 1}
.
For such a place,
H^{2}(F_{1,v_{0}},\mathrm{ad}^{0}\overline{\rho})=0
and the rational prime below
v_{0}
splits in any quadratic subfield of
F
. We choose two such places
v_{0},v_{0}^{\prime}
of distinct residue characteristic and set
S=S^{\prime}\cup\{v_{0},v_{0}^{\prime}\}
. We are now in a situation where all the assumptions of Proposition
5.6.1
are satisfied. We deduce that
\rho|_{G_{F_{1}}}
is automorphic, and solvable descent
[
ACC+18
, Proposition 6.5.13]
completes the job. ∎

6.
Modularity of elliptic curves over CM fields

In this section, our goal is to combine Theorem
5.2
with the results of
[
AKT23
]
to prove the following:

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
F
be an imaginary CM number field with
\zeta_{5}\notin F
. Let
E/F
be an elliptic curve satisfying one of the following two conditions:

(1)

\overline{r}_{E,3}
is decomposed generic and
\overline{r}_{E,3}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{3})}}
is absolutely irreducible.

(2)

\overline{r}_{E,5}
is decomposed generic and
\overline{r}_{E,5}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{5})}}
is absolutely ireducible.

Then
E
is modular.

By ‘
E
is modular’, we mean that either
E
has CM, or there is a cuspidal, regular algebraic automorphic representation
\pi
of
\GL_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
which is regular algebraic of weight 0, with
r_{\pi,\iota}\cong r_{E,p}^{\vee}
for a prime
p
and an isomorphism
\iota:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{C}}
.

Before proving the theorem, we give some corollaries. These will be improved further in the next section in the special case when
F
is imaginary quadratic.

Corollary 6.1.1
.

Let
F
be an imaginary quadratic field. Let
E/F
be an elliptic curve satisfying one of the following two conditions:

(1)

\overline{r}_{E,3}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{3})}}
is absolutely irreducible.

(2)

\overline{r}_{E,5}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{5})}}
is absolutely ireducible.

Them
E
is modular.

Proof.

Combine Theorem
6.1
and Lemma
6.2.2
.
∎

Corollary 6.1.2
.

Let
F
be an imaginary CM field that is Galois over
\mathbb{Q}
and such that
\zeta_{5}\notin F
.
Then 100% of Weierstrass equations over
F
, ordered by their height, define a modular elliptic curve.

Proof.

It follows from
[
AN20
, Lemma 2.3]
that, if
F/\mathbb{Q}
is finite Galois and
E/F
is an elliptic curve such
that the image of
\overline{r}_{E,5}
contains
\mathrm{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})
,
then
\overline{r}_{E,5}
is decomposed generic. Note than the decomposed generic condition used in
loc. cit.

is more restrictive than and therefore implies the one we are using. When
the image of
\overline{r}_{E,5}
contains
\mathrm{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})
, we also have that

\overline{r}_{E,5}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{5})}}
is absolutely ireducible, so the hypotheses of the second part of Theorem
6.1

are satisfied.

To conclude, we observe that a quantitative version of Hilbert irreducibility, see for example
[
Zyw10
, Prop. 5.2]
, implies
that 100% of elliptic curves

E
over a fixed number field
F
have the property that the image of
\overline{r}_{E,5}

contains
\mathrm{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})
.
∎

We recall a useful lemma from
[
AKT23
]
, which is proved using Varma’s results on local-global compatibility
[
Var24
]
.

Lemma 6.1.3
.

Let
F
be a CM field and let
E/F
be a modular elliptic curve without CM, with
r_{\pi,\iota}\cong r_{E,p}^{\vee}
for some choice of prime
p
and isomorphism
\iota:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{C}}
. Then:

(1)

\pi
has trivial central character and weight
0
, and is uniquely determined by
E
.

(2)

For every prime
p
and isomorphism
\iota:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, there is an isomorphism
r_{\pi,\iota}\cong r_{E,p}^{\vee}
.

(3)

For every isomorphism
\iota:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and finite place
v\nmid p
of
F
, there is an isomorphism
WD(r_{E,p}^{\vee}|_{G_{F_{v}}})^{F-ss}\cong\mathrm{rec}_{F_{v}}^{T}(\pi_{v})
.

(4)

Suppose
v|p
is a place where
E
has potentially multiplicative reduction. Then
\pi
is
\iota
-ordinary of weight
0
at
v
for any
\iota:\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

The first three parts are contained in
[
AKT23
, Lemma 9.1]
. The final part is proved in the same way as
[
AKT23
, Corollary 9.2]
: applying the third part to
r_{E,l}^{\vee}
for some
l\neq p
, we see that
\pi_{v}
is a twist of the Steinberg representation by a quadratic character (quadratic since the central character of
\pi_{v}
is trivial). Then the proof of
[
Ger19
, Lemma 5.6]
shows that
\pi_{v}
is
\iota
-ordinary at
v
.
∎

Here is another useful lemma, taken from the proof of
[
AKT23
, Corollary 9.14]
.

Lemma 6.1.4
.

Let
F
be a CM field with
\zeta_{5}\notin F
and let
E/F
be an elliptic curve such that the projective image of
\overline{r}_{E,5}(G_{F(\zeta_{5})})
is conjugate to
\PSL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})
. Then the extension of
F
cut out by the projective image of
\overline{r}_{E,5}(G_{F})
does not contain
\zeta_{5}
.

Proof.

The group
\overline{r}_{E,5}(G_{F(\zeta_{5})})
is a subgroup of
\SL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})
surjecting onto
\PSL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})
, so it is equal to
\SL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})
. We let
G
be the kernel of the map from
G_{F}
to the projective image of
\overline{r}_{E,5}(G_{F})
and let
H\subset G
be the kernel of the map from
G_{F(\zeta_{5})}
to the projective image of
\overline{r}_{E,5}(G_{F(\zeta_{5})})
. The extension of
F
cut out by the projective image of
\overline{r}_{E,5}(G_{F})
contains
\zeta_{5}
if and only if
H=G
. Since
\zeta_{5}\notin F
,
\det\overline{\rho}
has order
2
or
4
. In the first case, the projective image of
\overline{r}_{E,5}(G_{F})
is again
\PSL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})
so
[G:H]=[F(\zeta_{5}):F]>1
. In the second case,
[F(\zeta_{5}):F]>[\PGL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5}):\PSL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})]
which again means
H\neq G
.
∎

Now we state a variant of
[
AKT23
, Proposition 9.13]
:

Proposition 6.1.5
.

Let
F
be an imaginary CM number field, and let

\overline{\rho}:G_{F}\to\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{5})

be a continuous homomorphism with determinant
\bar{\epsilon}_{5}
. We assume
\overline{\rho}
is decomposed generic.

Suppose we have a decomposition
S_{5}=S_{5}^{st}\coprod S_{5}^{\ord}\coprod S_{5}^{\mathrm{ss}}
of the set of places in
F
dividing
5
.

Let
F^{\mathrm{avoid}}/F
be a finite Galois extension. Then we can find a solvable CM extension
L/F
and an elliptic curve
E/L
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

E
is modular.

(2)

The extension
L/F
is linearly disjoint from
F^{\mathrm{avoid}}/F

(3)

For each place
v|5
in
F
and
w|v
in
L
,
E_{F_{w}}
has good ordinary reduction if
v\in S_{5}^{\ord}
, good supersingular reduction if
v\in S_{5}^{\mathrm{ss}}
and (split) multiplicative reduction if
v\in S_{5}^{st}
.

(4)

There is an isomorphism
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{L}}\cong\overline{r}_{E,5}
.

(5)

\overline{\rho}|_{G_{L}}
is decomposed generic.

Proof.

We choose
L/F
to be a solvable CM extension such that:

•

For each place
w|2,3,5
of
L
,
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{L_{w}}}
is trivial and
w
is split over
L^{+}
.

•

For
w|5
, there are elliptic curves
E^{\ord}/L_{w}
,
E^{\mathrm{ss}}/L_{w}
with good ordinary and good supersingular reduction respectively, and trivial action of
G_{L_{w}}
on their
5
-torsion.

•

For each place
w|2
of
L
, the extension
L_{w}(\sqrt{-1})/L_{w}
is unramified.

•

L/F
is linearly disjoint from
F^{\mathrm{avoid}}/F
.

•

There is a prime
q>5
which is decomposed generic for
\overline{\rho}
and splits in
L
.

Now we apply
[
AKT23
, Lemma 9.7]
in the same way as in the proof of
[
AKT23
, Proposition 9.13]
to find an
L
-rational point of the modular curve
Y_{\overline{\rho}}
corresponding to a modular elliptic curve
E/L
. The curve
Y_{\overline{\rho}}
is isomorphic to an open subset of the projective line over
F
. Combining Hilbert irreducibility, in the form of
[
Ser08
, Theorem 3.5.3]
, and weak approximation for the projective line, we can find points in
Y_{\overline{\rho}}(L)
that avoid a thin subset (
[
Ser08
, Definition 3.1.1]
) and lie in specified non-empty
w
-adically open subsets
\Omega_{w}\subset Y_{\overline{\rho}}(L_{w})
for a finite set of places
w
. Compared to
[
AKT23
, Proposition 9.13]
, we replace the condition that
E_{L_{w}}
is a Tate curve for each place
w|5
with the condition that for
w
lying over a place in
S_{5}^{\ord}
,
E_{L_{w}}
has good ordinary reduction, for
w
lying over a place in
S_{5}^{\mathrm{ss}}
,
E_{L_{w}}
has good supersingular reduction and for
w
lying over a place in
S_{5}^{st}
,
E_{L_{w}}
is a Tate curve. Note that the modularity of
E
is then proved by applying
[
AKT23
, Proposition 9.12]
, which is not sensitive to the
5
-adic properties of
E
.
∎

We have a similar statement for mod
3
representations, which can be proved in the same way as
[
AKT23
, Proposition 9.15]
:

Proposition 6.1.6
.

Let
F
be an imaginary CM number field with
\zeta_{5}\notin F
, and let

\overline{\rho}:G_{F}\to\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{3})

be a continuous homomorphism with determinant
\bar{\epsilon}_{3}
. We assume
\overline{\rho}
is decomposed generic.

Suppose we have a decomposition
S_{3}=S_{3}^{st}\coprod S_{3}^{\ord}\coprod S_{3}^{\mathrm{ss}}
of the set of places in
F
dividing
3
.

Let
F^{\mathrm{avoid}}/F
be a finite Galois extension. Then we can find a solvable CM extension
L/F
and an elliptic curve
E/L
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

E
is modular.

(2)

The extension
L/F
is linearly disjoint from
F^{\mathrm{avoid}}/F

(3)

For each place
v|3
in
F
and
w|v
in
L
,
E_{F_{w}}
has good ordinary reduction if
v\in S_{3}^{\ord}
, good supersingular reduction if
v\in S_{3}^{\mathrm{ss}}
and and (split) multiplicative reduction if
v\in S_{3}^{st}
.

(4)

There is an isomorphism
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{L}}\cong\overline{r}_{E,3}
.

(5)

\overline{\rho}|_{G_{L}}
is decomposed generic.

Lemma 6.1.7
.

Let
E
be the modular elliptic curve produced by Proposition
6.1.5
or Proposition
6.1.6
with
r_{E,p}^{\vee}\cong r_{\iota}(\pi)
for
p=3
or
5
respectively. Let
w|p
be a place of
L
, lying over the place
v
of
F
. Then
\pi
is
\iota
-ordinary at
w
if
v\in S_{p}^{st}
. The local factor
\pi_{w}
is unramified if
v\in S_{p}^{\ord}\coprod S_{p}^{\mathrm{ss}}
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
6.1.3
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Choose
p\in\{3,5\}
so that
\overline{r}_{E,p}
is decomposed generic and
\overline{r}_{E,p}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
is absolutely irreducible. Now we apply
Proposition
6.1.5
or
6.1.6
with
\overline{\rho}=\overline{r}_{E,p}
,
S_{p}^{\ord}
the set of places above
p
where
E
has potentially good ordinary reduction,
S_{p}^{\mathrm{ss}}
the set of places where
E
has potentially good supersingular reduction and
S_{p}^{st}
the set of places where
E
has potentially multiplicative reduction. The appropriate proposition gives us a solvable extension
L/F
and a modular elliptic curve
A/L
with
\overline{r}_{E,p}|_{G_{L}}\cong\overline{r}_{A,p}
, such that the hypotheses of Theorem
5.2
apply to
\rho=r_{E,p}^{\vee}|_{G_{L}}
(we use Lemma
6.1.7
here). The assumption that
\zeta_{5}\notin F
is sufficient to check the condition on the projective image of
\overline{\rho}
in the theorem, by Lemma
6.1.4
. We deduce that
E_{L}
is modular, and the modularity of
E
follows by solvable descent.
∎

6.2.
Group theory

We now do a little bit of group theory to optimise the statement of Theorem
6.1
when
F
is quadratic (cf. Corollary
6.1.1
). Our main tool will be the following well-known lemma:

Lemma 6.2.1

(Goursat’s lemma)
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be finite groups and suppose
H\subset G_{1}\times G_{2}
is a subgroup with the projection map
p_{i}:H\to G_{i}
surjective for
i=1
and
2
. Then we have normal subgroups
N_{i}=H\cap G_{i}\subset G_{i}
and an isomorphism
\phi:G_{1}/N_{1}\cong G_{2}/N_{2}
such that

H=\{(g_{1},g_{2}):\phi(g_{1}N_{1})=g_{2}N_{2}\}.

Lemma 6.2.2
.

Let
F/{\mathbb{Q}}
be a quadratic field, let
p
be an odd prime, and let
\overline{\rho}:G_{F}\to\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
be a homomorphism. Suppose that
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
is absolutely irreducible. Then
\overline{\rho}
is decomposed generic.

Proof.

We consider the homomorphism
P=\mathrm{Proj}(\overline{\rho}):G_{F}\to\PGL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
. Set
L=\overline{F}^{\ker P}
, so
P
factors through an embedding
P:\Gal(L/F)\hookrightarrow\PGL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
. We also set
L_{1}=\overline{F}^{\ker P\cap G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}=L(\zeta_{p})
.

Let
\widetilde{L}
be the Galois closure of
L
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
in
\overline{F}
. Fixing a lift
c\in\Gal(\widetilde{L}/{\mathbb{Q}})
of the non-trivial element in
\Gal(F/{\mathbb{Q}})
, we have an injective map:

\displaystyle\Gal(\widetilde{L}/F)

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\Gal(L/F)\times\Gal(L/F)

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mapsto(\sigma|_{L},(c^{-1}\sigma c)|_{L})

whose composition with each of the two projection maps to
\Gal(L/F)
is surjective. Injectivity follows from the fact that
\widetilde{L}
is the composite of
L
and
c(L)
.

The Galois closure
\widetilde{L_{1}}
of
L_{1}
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
is
\widetilde{L}(\zeta_{p})
, and restricting the above map to the subgroup
\Gal(\widetilde{L_{1}}/F(\zeta_{p}))=\Gal(\widetilde{L}/\widetilde{L}\cap F(\zeta_{p}))
gives an injective map

\Gal(\widetilde{L_{1}}/F(\zeta_{p}))\hookrightarrow\Gal(L_{1}/F(\zeta_{p}))\times\Gal(L_{1}/F(\zeta_{p}))

whose composition with each of the two projection maps to
\Gal(L_{1}/F(\zeta_{p}))
is surjective.

We are going to show that there is an element
\tau\in\Gal(\widetilde{L_{1}}/F(\zeta_{p}))
whose image under each projection map to
\Gal(L_{1}/F(\zeta_{p}))
is a non-identity element of order prime to
p
. First we need to show that
\Gal(L_{1}/F(\zeta_{p}))
itself contains a non-identity element of order prime to
p
. This group contains the image of
\overline{\rho}(G_{F}(\zeta_{p}))
in
\PGL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
. The irreducibility of
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
implies that
\overline{\rho}(G_{F}(\zeta_{p}))
either has order prime to
p
or contains
\SL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
(by Dickson’s classification, or, more simply,
[
Ser72
, Proposition 15]
). It follows that we can find a non-identity element
T\in\Gal(L_{1}/F(\zeta_{p}))
of order prime to
p
.

We now denote
\Gal(\widetilde{L_{1}}/F(\zeta_{p}))
by
H
and
\Gal({L_{1}}/F(\zeta_{p}))
by
G
. Goursat’s lemma tells us that there are normal subgroups
N_{1},N_{2}\triangleleft G
and an isomorphism
\phi:G/N_{1}\cong G/N_{2}
such that
H=\{(g_{1},g_{2}):\phi(g_{1}N_{1})=g_{2}N_{2}\}
. Note that
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are necessarily of the same order. We separate into two cases:

•

The
N_{i}
are
p
-groups (we include the possibility that the
N_{i}
are trivial). In this case, we fix a lift
T^{\prime}\in G
of
\phi(TN_{1})\in G/N_{2}
. The order of
\phi(TN_{1})
is equal to the order of
T
, and is equal to the order of
T^{\prime}
up to a
p
-power factor. So, replacing
T
and
T^{\prime}
by a sufficiently large
p
th power if necessary, we have
(T,T^{\prime})\in H
with
T
and
T^{\prime}
non-identity elements with order prime to
p
.

•

The
N_{i}
are not
p
-groups. Then we can let
T_{1}
be a non-identity element of
N_{1}
of order prime to
p
and
T_{2}
a non-identity element of
N_{2}
of order prime to
p
. The element
(T_{1},T_{2})
is contained in
H
.

We have now constructed the desired element
\tau\in\Gal(\widetilde{L_{1}}/F(\zeta_{p}))
. By Chebotarev density, we can choose a rational prime
l
, unramified in
\widetilde{L_{1}}
, such that
\mathrm{Frob}_{l}
is the conjugacy class of
\tau
in
\Gal(\widetilde{L_{1}}/{\mathbb{Q}})
. Since
\tau
fixes
F(\zeta_{p})
, we have
l\equiv 1
mod
p
and
l
splits completely in
F
. The Frobenius elements
\mathrm{Frob}_{v}
for
v|l
in
F
are given by the
\Gal(\widetilde{L_{1}}/F)
-conjugacy classes contained in
\mathrm{Frob}_{l}
. These are the
\Gal(\widetilde{L_{1}}/F)
-conjugacy classes of
\tau
and
c^{-1}\tau c
(which could coincide).

By construction,
\overline{\rho}(\tau)
and
\overline{\rho}(c^{-1}\tau c)
have image in
\PGL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
a non-identity element of order prime to
p
. It follows that they are both regular semisimple elements of
\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
. Since
l\equiv 1
mod
p
, we have shown that
l
is a decomposed generic prime for
\overline{\rho}
.
∎

7.
Quadratic points on modular curves and modularity over quadratic fields

In this section, inspired by the proof of modularity of elliptic curves over real quadratic fields
[
FLHS15
]
, our goal is to extend Corollary
6.1.1
to cover many of the excluded cases where
r_{E,p}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
is absolutely reducible for
p=3
and
5
. We first state the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
F
be an imaginary quadratic field, and let
E/F
be an elliptic curve such that one of the following conditions holds:

(1)

The action of
G_{F}
on
E[5]
is irreducible (not necessarily absolutely irreducible).

(2)

The action of
G_{F}
on
E[3]
is irreducible and the image of
G_{F}
in
\Aut(E[3])
is not the normalizer of a split Cartan subgroup.

Then
E
is modular.

The following lemma helps explicate the condition that
r_{E,p}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
is absolutely reducible for
p=3
and
5
.

Lemma 7.1.1
.

Let
F
be a number field and
\overline{\rho}:G_{F}\to\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
an irreducible representation with determinant
\overline{\epsilon}_{p}
and
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
absolutely reducible.

(1)

\overline{\rho}(G_{F})
is a subgroup of the normalizer of a Cartan subgroup.

(2)

If
p=3
,
\overline{\rho}(G_{F})
is conjugate to
C_{\mathrm{s}}^{+}(3)
(the normalizer of a split Cartan subgroup of
\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{3})
) or to a subgroup of
C_{\mathrm{ns}}(3)
(a non-split Cartan).

(3)

If
p=5
and
[F(\zeta_{5}):F]=4
, then
\overline{\rho}(G_{F})
is conjugate to a subgroup of
C_{\mathrm{ns}}^{+}(5)
(the normalizer of a non-split Cartan).

Proof.

Let
G=\overline{\rho}(G_{F})
. If
G
acts absolutely reducibly on
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{2}
then it is a subgroup of a Cartan subgroup (
G
acts semisimply, because it acts irreducibly). Suppose
G
acts absolutely irreducibly. We show that
G
is a subgroup of the normalizer of a Cartan subgroup. We have
G^{\prime}=G\cap\SL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})=\overline{\rho}(G_{F(\zeta_{p})})
, and we apply
[
FLHS15
, Lemma 2.2]
to conclude the proof of the first part.

Now assume that
G
is absolutely irreducible and
\det(G)={\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}
. It follows from
[
FLHS15
, Lemma 2.2]
that, if
\GL_{2}^{+}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
is the subgroup of
\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
consisting of matrices with square determinant, then
G^{+}=G\cap\GL_{2}^{+}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
is contained in a Cartan subgroup
C
with
G
contained in the normalizer of
C
.

For the second part, if
F={\mathbb{Q}}(\zeta_{3})
, then
G=G^{\prime}
is absolutely reducible and is therefore a subgroup of a non-split Cartan. We can now assume
G
is absolutely irreducible and
\det(G)={\mathbb{F}}_{3}^{\times}
. Suppose
G^{\prime}
is contained in a non-split Cartan
C_{\mathrm{ns}}
and
G
is contained in its normalizer
C^{+}_{\mathrm{ns}}
. After conjugation, we can assume that

C_{\mathrm{ns}}=\{\begin{pmatrix}x&-y\\
y&x\end{pmatrix}:(x,y)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{3}^{2}-\{(0,0)\}.

Note that
C_{\mathrm{ns}}\cap\SL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{3})=\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle
is cyclic of order 4 and is also contained in the normalizer of the diagonal split Cartan
C_{\mathrm{s}}(3)
. If
G^{\prime}
is contained in the scalars, then
G
is the pre-image of a single order
2
element in
\PGL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
and is therefore absolutely reducible. So
G^{\prime}=C_{\mathrm{ns}}\cap\SL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{3})
. Considering the possibilities for
G\subset C_{\mathrm{ns}}^{+}
with
G\subsetneq C_{\mathrm{ns}}
which contain
G^{\prime}
with index
2
, we can see that
G
must also be contained in the normalizer
C^{+}_{\mathrm{s}}(3)
of
C_{\mathrm{s}}(3)
. The irreducible proper subgroups of
C^{+}_{\mathrm{s}}(3)
are also contained in
C_{\mathrm{ns}}(3)
.

Finally, suppose
p=5
and that
G^{+}
is contained in the diagonal split Cartan
C_{s}(5)
with
G
contained in
C^{+}_{s}(5)
. We assume that
G
is irreducible (not necessarily absolutely irreducible). If
G^{+}
contains an element with eigenvalues
1
and
-1
, then we are in the situation of (the proof of)
[
FLHS15
, Proposition 4.1(b)]
, which shows that
G
is a subgroup (of index 3) in the normalizer of a non-split Cartan. So we assume that
G^{+}
does not contain such an element. It follows that
G^{+}
is equal to the subgroup of scalar matrices in
\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
, so
G
is the pre-image of an order
2
element in
\PGL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
. We conclude that
G
is absolutely reducible, hence a subgroup of a non-split Cartan.
∎

At this point we need to introduce modular curves with special level structures at
3
and
5
. We follow the notation of
[
FLHS15
, §2.2]
, so for a subgroup
H\subset\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
containing
-I
and with
\det(H)={\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}
, we have a modular curve
X(H)/{\mathbb{Q}}
equipped with its
j
-invariant map
X(H)\to X(1)
. If
H_{1}\subset\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p_{1}})
and
H_{2}\subset\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p_{2}})
with
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
,
X(H_{1},H_{2})
is the modular curve given by the normalization of the fibre product
X(H_{1})\times_{X(1)}X(H_{2})
. The cuspidal points of
X(H_{1},H_{2})
are those lying over
\infty\in X(1)
.

If
F\subset\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
is a number field, the non-cuspidal
F
-rational points of
X(H_{1},H_{2})
correspond to
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
-isomorphism classes of pairs
(E,[\eta])
, where
E
is an elliptic curve over
F
and
[\eta]
is an
(H_{1}\times H_{2})
-orbit of isomorphisms

\eta:\prod_{i=1}^{2}{\mathbb{F}}_{p_{i}}^{2}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{2}E[p_{i}](\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}})

such that
\eta^{-1}\overline{r}_{E,p}(G_{F})\eta
is contained in
H_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

We will be concerned with the subgroups
\mathrm{b}p
,
\mathrm{s}p
,
\mathrm{ns}p
which are respectively the upper triangular Borel,
C_{\mathrm{s}}^{+}(p)
and
C_{\mathrm{ns}}^{+}(p)
in
\GL_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
. We will also need the Cartan subgroups themselves:
\mathrm{s}p^{\circ}:=C_{\mathrm{s}}(p)
and
\mathrm{ns}p^{\circ}:=C_{\mathrm{ns}}(p)
.

Before proving Theorem
7.1
, we give a corollary. Recall that
X_{0}(15)=X(\mathrm{b}3,\mathrm{b}5)
is an elliptic curve of rank zero over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. It is the curve with Cremona label 15A1 (see
[
FLHS15
, Lemma 5.6]
).

Corollary 7.1.2
.

Let
F
be an imaginary quadratic field such that
X_{0}(15)(F)
is finite. Then every elliptic curve
E/F
is modular.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.1
, we only need to consider
E/F
giving rise to an
F
-rational point
P
of
X_{0}(15)=X(\mathrm{b}3,\mathrm{b}5)
or
X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{b}5)
. We can assume that
E
does not have CM (otherwise it would be modular), so if
(E^{\prime},[\eta])
also gives the point
P
then
E^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
E
or a quadratic twist of
E
. Now it suffices to show that each of the
j
-invariants coming from points of
X_{0}(15)(F)
and
X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{b}5)(F)
are modular. The curve
X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{b}5)
is an ellipic curve, with Cremona label 15A3 (
[
FLHS15
, Lemma 5.7]
), and is isogenous to
X_{0}(15)
. We are assuming that
X_{0}(15)(F)
is finite, so
X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{b}5)(F)
is also finite. It remains to check modularity for the non-cuspidal torsion points defined over imaginary quadratic fields.

We have
X_{0}(15)({\mathbb{Q}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/4{\mathbb{Z}}
. A Legendre form for
X_{0}(15)/{\mathbb{Q}}
is
y^{2}=x(x+16)(x+25)
. It follows from
[
Kwo97
, Theorem 1]
that the only quadratic fields
F
with
X_{0}(15)({\mathbb{Q}})\subsetneq X_{0}(15)(F)^{\mathrm{tors}}
are
F={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-1})
and
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{5})
. This information is also contained in the LMFDB. It remains to show that elliptic curves giving rise to the 8 points in
X_{0}(15)({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-1}))\backslash X_{0}(15)({\mathbb{Q}})
are modular. This reduces to checking modularity of a single elliptic curve (isogenous and conjugate curves give the other points), and its modularity can be verified using the Faltings–Serre method
[
DGP10
]
which has been carried out as part of the LMFDB project
[
LMF22
,
Elliptic curve 4050.1-c3 over number field
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-1})
]
.

We also have
X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{b}5)({\mathbb{Q}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/4{\mathbb{Z}}
. A Legendre form for
X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{b}5)/{\mathbb{Q}}
is
y^{2}=x(x+1)(x+16)
, and it follows from
[
Kwo97
, Theorem 1]
that the only quadratic field
F
with
X_{0}(15)({\mathbb{Q}})\subsetneq X_{0}(15)(F)^{\mathrm{tors}}
is
F={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{5})
(again, this information is contained in the LMFDB).
∎

Combining Theorem
6.1
with Lemma
7.1.1
, to prove Theorem
7.1
we need to show modularity of the elliptic curves defined over imaginary quadratic fields giving rise to points of the following modular curves:

(1)

X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{b}5)

(2)

X(\mathrm{b}3,\mathrm{ns}5)

(3)

X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{ns}5)

(4)

X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{ns}5)

We will do this is the following subsections. We used Magma to do the computations
[
BCP97
]
, and there are associated Magma files available at
https://github.com/jjmnewton/modularity-iqf
.

To help us find equations for these curves, we use the following well-known facts about ‘small’ modular curves:

Proposition 7.1.3
.

(1)

The curve
X(\mathrm{b}3)
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
with co-ordinate
x
and
j
-invariant
\frac{(x+27)(x+3)^{3}}{x}
.

(2)

The curve
X(\mathrm{b}5)
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
with co-ordinate
x
and
j
-invariant
\frac{(x^{2}+250x+5^{5})^{3}}{x^{5}}
. The Fricke involution
w_{5}
is given by
x\mapsto 5^{3}/x
.

(3)

The curve
X(\mathrm{ns}3)
is isomorphic
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
with co-ordinate
x
and
j
-invariant
x^{3}
.

(4)

The curve
X(\mathrm{ns}5)
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
with co-ordinate
x
and
j
-invariant
\frac{5^{3}x(2x+1)^{3}(2x^{2}+7x+8)^{3}}{(x^{2}+x-1)^{5}}
.

(5)

The curve
X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
is an elliptic curve over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, isomorphic to the curve with Cremona label
225A1
, with Mordell–Weil group
X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)({\mathbb{Q}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(6)

The curve
X(\mathrm{s}3)
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
with co-ordinate
x
and
j
-invariant
\frac{3^{3}(x+1)^{3}(x-3)^{3}}{x^{3}}

Proof.

For the first and last part, see
[
SZ17
, Table 1]
.
For the second part, see
[
McM04
, Table 3]
. For the third and fourth parts, see
[
Che99
, Proposition 5.1, Corollary 6.3]
. For the fifth part, it follows from the third and fourth parts that a singular model for
X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
is given by the equation
x^{3}=\frac{5^{3}y(2y+1)^{3}(2y^{2}+7y+8)^{3}}{(y^{2}+y-1)^{5}}
. Some simple manipulations show that this is birational to the elliptic curve
y^{2}-y=x^{3}+1
, which has Cremona label
225A1
as claimed. This is also checked in the Magma file
ns3ns5-elliptic.m
.
∎

7.2.
Quadratic points on
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{b}5)

We begin by specifying an equation for the modular curve
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{b}5)
, together with the properties we will need to show modularity of quadratic points.

Proposition 7.2.1
.

(1)

A model for
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{b}5)
is given by the genus one curve

C:y^{2}=-3(x^{4}+2x^{3}-x^{2}+10x+25),

where (the homogeneization of) this equation defines a smooth curve in the weighted projective space
\mathbb{P}(1,2,1)
.

(2)

With the above equation,
w_{5}
transforms
x
-co-ordinates by
x\mapsto 5/x
.

(3)

The Jacobian
\mathrm{Jac}_{C}
is isomorphic to the elliptic curve with Cremona label
45A2
, and in particular has
\mathrm{Jac}_{C}({\mathbb{Q}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

For the first part, we proceed in a similar way to the proof of
[
Zyw15
, Lemma 4.4]
. It follows from Proposition
7.1.3
that
X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{b}5)
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
, with
j
-invariant given by
j(x)=\frac{(x^{6}+250x^{3}+5^{5})^{3}}{x^{15}}
and
w_{5}
given by
x\mapsto 5/x
. The map
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{b}5)\to X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{b}5)
has degree two, and is ramified at 4 points with
j
-invariant 1728. The fibre of the
j
-invariant map on
X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{b}5)
at 1728 is cut out by the vanishing of the polynomial

(x^{6}+250x^{3}+5^{5})^{3}-1728x^{15}\\
=(x^{2}-5x-25)^{2}(x^{2}-2x+5)(x^{4}+2x^{3}-x^{2}+10x+25)(x^{4}+5x^{3}+50x^{2}-125x+625)^{2}.

It follows that the ramification locus is cut out by either
(x^{2}-5x-25)^{2}
or
x^{4}+2x^{3}-x^{2}+10x+25
. We claim that it is the latter. If we are in the former case, then we have an elliptic curve
E/{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{5})
with
j
-invariant
1728
and image of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{5})}
on
\Aut(E[3])
contained in a non-split Cartan subgroup. Since
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{5})
is totally real, this image also contains an element conjugate to
\mathrm{diag}(1,-1)
but non-split Cartans mod
3
contain no such elements.

We conclude that
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{b}5)
is defined by an equation

y^{2}=c(x^{4}+2x^{3}-x^{2}+10x+25)

for some squarefree
c\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. We claim that
c=-3
. We see from the equation (since the constant term
25
is a square) that
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{c})
is the field of definition of the cusps of
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{b}5)
, or equivalently the field of definition of the cusps of
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ})
. It is well-known that this field is
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-3})
. Here is one justification: by ramification considerations as above, the double cover
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ})\to X(\mathrm{ns}3)
is given by
y^{2}=d(x^{2}+12x+144)
for a squarefree
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The unique rational point of
X(\mathrm{ns}3)
with
j
-invariant 1728 is
x=12
. The fibre over this point in
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ})
is given by
y^{2}=432d
, so
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3d})
is determined by the image of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3d})}
in
\Aut(E[3])
being contained in a non-split Cartan, where
E/{\mathbb{Q}}
has
j
-invariant 1728 (hence CM by
{\mathbb{Z}}[\sqrt{-1}]
). It follows that
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{3d})={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-1})
and we deduce that
d=-3
.

Having computed a model for our genus one curve, the Jacobian can be identified using classical invariant theory and the Mordell–Weil group determined with the help of a two-descent (see
[
AKM+01
]
for equations for the Jacobian, we did the calculations using Magma as documented in the file
ns3ob5.m
).
∎

We will make use of the following points in
C({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-3}))
:

\displaystyle\infty^{+}

\displaystyle=(1:\sqrt{-3}:0)

\displaystyle\infty^{-}

\displaystyle=(1:-\sqrt{-3}:0)

\displaystyle 0^{+}

\displaystyle=(0:5\sqrt{-3}:1)

\displaystyle P_{1}

\displaystyle=(-2:-\sqrt{-3}:1)

\displaystyle P_{2}

\displaystyle=(-5/2:5\sqrt{-3}/4:1).

We have
C({\mathbb{Q}})=\emptyset
, as can be checked
3
-adically. Using the isomorphism between
C_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-3})}
and
\mathrm{Jac}_{C,{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-3})}
given by
P\mapsto[P]-[\infty^{-}]
, we can match up the order
2
rational points of
\mathrm{Jac}_{C}
with the equivalence classes of the divisors:

\displaystyle D_{0}

\displaystyle=0^{+}-\infty^{-}

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=P_{1}-\infty^{-}

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=P_{2}-\infty^{-}.

At this point, we want to compute which points of
\mathrm{Jac}_{C}({\mathbb{Q}})
are representented by rational divisors, not just rational equivalence classes. We denote the group of degree 0 rational divisors modulo linear equivalence by
\Pic^{0}(C)
.

Proposition 7.2.2
.

\Pic^{0}(C)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
, with generator
[0^{+}-\infty^{-}]
.

Proof.

Our computations here are again documented in the file
ns3ob5.m
. There is an injective map
\Pic^{0}(C)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Jac}_{C}({\mathbb{Q}})
, so we need to determine which of the divisors
D_{i}
(for
i=0,1,2
) are linearly equivalent to a rational divisor. For this, we essentially follow the procedure outlined in
[
BF04
]
. Translating by the rational divisor
\infty^{+}+\infty^{-}
, we consider the degree
2
divisors
E_{i}=D_{i}+\infty^{+}+\infty^{-}
. For each divisor, we compute a basis
\{1,f_{i}\}
for each of the two-dimensional Riemann–Roch spaces
L(E_{i})
defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-3})
. We get:

\displaystyle f_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{y+(\sqrt{-3}x^{2}+5\sqrt{-3})y}{x}

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{y+(\sqrt{-3}x^{2}-5\sqrt{-3})y}{x+2}

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{y+(\sqrt{-3}x^{2}-5\sqrt{-3})y}{x+5/2}.

A divisor class
[E_{i}]
contains a rational divisor if and only if there is an effective degree
2
rational divisor
D^{\prime}_{i}=P+\sigma(P)
linearly equivalent to
E_{i}
, with
P\in C(F)
for a quadratic field
F
and
\sigma
the non-trivial element of
\Gal(F/{\mathbb{Q}})
. If this is the case, then
D^{\prime}_{i}
is the divisor of zeroes of a meromorphic function
f_{i}-\alpha
for some
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-3})
. For
i=0,1,2
respectively this gives us equations:

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\alpha x-(\sqrt{-3}x^{2}+5\sqrt{-3})

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\alpha(x+2)-(\sqrt{-3}x^{2}-5\sqrt{-3})

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\alpha(x+5/2)-(\sqrt{-3}x^{2}-5\sqrt{-3}).

Squaring both sides, using the equation for
C
, and dividing out by the linear factor in
x
(which doesn’t correspond to a zero of
f_{i}-\alpha
) gives us equations:

(7.2.1)

(6-2\sqrt{-3}\alpha)x^{2}+(\alpha^{2}-33)x+(30-10\sqrt{-3}\alpha)=0

(7.2.2)

(6-2\sqrt{-3}\alpha)x^{2}+(\alpha^{2}+15)x+(2\alpha^{2}+10\sqrt{-3}\alpha)=0

(7.2.3)

(6-2\sqrt{-3}\alpha)x^{2}+(\alpha^{2}+12)x+(5\alpha^{2}/2+10\sqrt{-3}\alpha)=0.

Equation (
7.2.1
) has the solution
x=1+2i
,
\alpha=3+2\sqrt{-3}
, and
E_{0}
is linearly equivalent to
P+\sigma(P)
for
P=(1+2i:3+6i:1)
. Note that, since (
7.2.1
) rescales to
x^{2}+\cdots+5=0
, we have
x(P)x(\sigma(P))=5
for any
P
defined over a quadratic field with
E_{0}\sim P+\sigma(P)
.

For the zeroes of Equation (
7.2.2
) to come from a rational divisor, we must have
\frac{\alpha^{2}+15}{6-2\sqrt{-3}\alpha}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. Writing
\alpha=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}\sqrt{-3}
, with
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, we deduce that
\alpha_{0}=0
or
\alpha_{0}^{2}+3\alpha_{1}^{2}+6\alpha_{1}+15=0
. The case
\alpha_{0}=0
can be excluded by considering the equation for
y
in terms of
x
. The equation
\alpha_{0}^{2}+3\alpha_{1}^{2}+6\alpha_{1}+15=0
has no rational solutions (check
3
-adically). The associated conic is the Brauer–Severi variety associated to the divisor class
[E_{1}]
, as described in
[
BF04
]
.

Similarly, from Equation (
7.2.3
) we get the conic equation
\alpha_{0}^{2}+3\alpha_{1}^{2}+6\alpha_{1}+12=0
, which again has no rational points (check
3
-adically).
∎

Remark 7.2.3
.

The conic associated to the divisor
E_{0}
has equation
\alpha_{0}^{2}+3\alpha_{1}^{2}+6\alpha_{1}-33=0
, which does have rational points (for example
(3,2)
, which corresponds to
\alpha=3+2\sqrt{-3}
).

Proposition 7.2.4
.

Suppose
P\in C(F)
for a quadratic field
F
. We assume
P
is not one of the two points at infinity. Then either
x(P)\in{\mathbb{Q}}
or
x(P)x(\sigma(P))=5
, where
\sigma
is the non-trivial element of
\Gal(F/{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Proof.

We have
[P+\sigma(P)-\infty^{+}-\infty^{-}]=0
or
[0^{+}-\infty^{-}]
in
\Pic^{0}(C)
, so we deduce that
[P+\sigma(P)]=[\infty^{+}+\infty^{-}]
or
[0^{+}+\infty^{+}]
. In the second case, the proof of the previous proposition shows that
x(P)x(\sigma(P))=5
. In the first case, we have
P+\sigma(P)=\infty^{+}+\infty^{-}+\mathrm{div}(f)
, where
f\in L(\infty^{+}+\infty^{-})
is a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear combination of
1
and
x
. In particular,
P
is a zero of
x-\alpha
for some
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, so
x(P)\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.
∎

Corollary 7.2.5
.

Let
F
be a quadratic field and
E/F
elliptic curve such that
E
is the elliptic curve underlying an
F
-point
P\in X((\mathrm{ns3})^{\circ},\mathrm{b5})(F)
. Then
E
is modular.

Proof.

According to Proposition
7.2.4
we have two cases. In the first case,
P
maps to a point in
X(\mathrm{ns3},\mathrm{b5})({\mathbb{Q}})
. In particular,
E
has rational
j
-invariant and is therefore modular.

In the second case,
P
maps to a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-point of the Atkin–Lehner quotient
X(\mathrm{ns3},\mathrm{b5})/w_{5}
. This shows that
E
and
E^{\sigma}
are
5
-isogenous over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
, where
\sigma
is the non-trivial element of
\Gal(F/{\mathbb{Q}})
. In particular,
E
is a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-curve, and is therefore modular (cf.
[
FLHS15
, §12]
).
∎

7.3.
Quadratic points on
X(\mathrm{b}3,\mathrm{ns}5)

For this curve, our approach is similar to that of
[
FLHS15
, Lemma 5.1]
. Our computations are documented in the file
b3ns5.m
.

Proposition 7.3.1
.

(1)

X(\mathrm{b}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
is a curve of genus 2, with hyperelliptic equation

C:y^{2}=9x^{6}-6x^{5}-35x^{4}+40x^{2}+12x-8.

(2)

The hyperelliptic involution on
X(\mathrm{b}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
is equal to the Atkin–Lehner involution
w_{3}
.

(3)

The Mordell–Weil group
\mathrm{Jac}(C)({\mathbb{Q}})
of the Jacobian of
C
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/10{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

We used our description for
X(\mathrm{b}3)
and
X(\mathrm{ns}5)
to write down (an affine patch of) the fibre product
X(\mathrm{b}3)\times_{X(1)}X(\mathrm{ns}5)
and then used the Magma routines
IsHyperelliptic
and
SimplifiedModel
to find the displayed equation for
X(\mathrm{b}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
, together with a birational map from
X(\mathrm{b}3)\times_{X(1)}X(\mathrm{ns}5)
to
C
.

To show that
w_{3}
is the hyperelliptic involution, it suffices to show that it has (at least) 6 fixed points. Alternatively, we can compute the automorphism group of
X(\mathrm{b}3,\mathrm{ns}5)

[
LSR21
]
and note that it has order two, so the only non-trivial automorphism is the hyperelliptic involution. The fixed points of
w_{3}
come from the elliptic curves
E_{0}={\mathbb{C}}/{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-3})}
and
E_{1}={\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{Z}}[\sqrt{-3}]
with
\mathrm{b}3
level structure coming from the kernel of the multiplication by
\sqrt{-3}
map; using the moduli description of
[
KP16
]
we can check that there are indeed 6 fixed points for
w_{3}
, 2 from
E_{0}
and 4 from
E_{1}
.

We computed the Mordell–Weil group using the Magma routine
MordellWeilGroupGenus2
. For our curve, a two-descent suffices to prove that the Jacobian has rank 0. We understand that this routine was created by Michael Stoll, see also
[
Sto01
]
. To double check that the torsion subgroup has size at most 20, we can compute that
\mathrm{Jac}_{C}({\mathbb{F}}_{7})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/20{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathrm{Jac}(C)({\mathbb{Q}})[2]
has size 4 (
\mathrm{Jac}(C)(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}})[2]
is given by
(x_{1},0)-(x_{2},0)
where the
x_{i}
are roots of
9x^{6}-6x^{5}-35x^{4}+40x^{2}+12x-8
; this polynomial has 3 rational roots and an irreducible cubic factor over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, whence it follows that there are 4 rational 2-torsion points).
∎

We write
D_{\infty}
for the degree two (hyperelliptic) divisor at
\infty
for
C
.

Lemma 7.3.2
.

Let
P
be a quadratic point of
C
, with conjugate
\sigma(P)
. Then we are in one of the two following cases:

(1)

[P+\sigma(P)]=[D_{\infty}]
in
\Pic(C)
. Then
P=(x,\pm\sqrt{f(x)})
where
f(x)
is the polynomial defining
C
, with
x\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.

(2)

[P+\sigma(P)-D_{\infty}]
is non-zero in
\Pic(C)
. The divisor
P+\sigma(P)
is the unique degree 2 effective divisor representing its divisor class.

Proof.

Note that
[D_{\infty}]
is the canonical divisor class. In the first case we have
L(D_{\infty})=\langle 1,x\rangle
so
P
and
\sigma(P)
are zeroes of
x-\alpha
for
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Q}}
. In the second case, by Riemann–Roch we have
\dim L(P+\sigma(P))=1
.
∎

Proposition 7.3.3
.

Suppose
P\in C(F)
for an imaginary quadratic field
F
. We assume
P
is not one of the two points at infinity. Then
x(P)\in{\mathbb{Q}}
or
F={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-11})
and, up to complex conjugation,
P=(\frac{-5+\sqrt{-11}}{6},\pm\frac{17-\sqrt{-11}}{6})
.

Proof.

By the previous lemma, it suffices to determine the non-zero elements
A\in\mathrm{Jac}(C)({\mathbb{Q}})
with
A+[D_{\infty}]
represented by an effective divisor with points in its support defined over an imaginary quadratic field. Magma returns elements of
\mathrm{Jac}(C)({\mathbb{Q}})
using the Mumford representation, which in particular gives a minimal polynomial for the
x
co-ordinate. So it is easy to see which points may be imaginary quadratic. There are 9 non-zero divisor classes supported on points with rational
x
co-ordinate, 2 supported at
\infty
, 2 supported on the imaginary quadratic points specified in the statement of this proposition (and their conjugates); the remaining 6 are supported on real quadratic points.
∎

Corollary 7.3.4
.

Let
F
be a quadratic field and
E/F
an elliptic curve such that
E
is the elliptic curve underlying an
F
-point
P\in X(\mathrm{b}3,\mathrm{ns}5)(F)
. Then
E
is modular.

Proof.

The points
P\in C(F)
with
x(P)\in{\mathbb{Q}}
(and the points at infinity) have
\sigma(P)=w_{3}(P)
. So in this case,
E
is a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-curve. We are now only concerned with the
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-11})
points identified in Proposition
7.3.3
. They are related by conjugation and
w_{3}
, so it suffices to show modularity of one of them. We conside the elliptic curve
E_{0}/{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-11})
described by
[
LMF22
,
Elliptic curve 8100.2-a2 over number field
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-11})
]
. On the one hand, it is modular, because the data in loc. cit. shows that the image of
G_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-11})}
on
\Aut(E_{0}[5])
is the full normalizer of a non-split Cartan, and therefore
E_{0}
satisfies the hypotheses of Corollary
6.1.1
(modularity was also checked explicitly by LMFDB using the Faltings–Serre method). On the other hand, we can write down a
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-11})
-rational point of
X(\mathrm{b}3)\times_{X(1)}X(\mathrm{ns}5)
with the same
j
-invariant as
E_{0}
, map it to
C
, and verify that we obtain one of our points of interest. We deduce that these points are also modular.
∎

7.4.
Quadratic points on
X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
and
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{ns}5)

Both curves
X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
and
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{ns}5)
are bi-elliptic, admitting degree two maps to
X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
. The quadratic points with image a rational point in
X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
are modular, so we need to understand quadratic points which are not pulled back from rational points. To do this, we use Siksek’s relative symmetric power Chabauty method
[
Sik09
]
. We can closely follow the implementation of this method by Box for some modular curves
X_{0}(N)

[
Box21
]
. Our computations here are documented in the Magma files
ns3ons5.m
and
s3ns5.m
.

We outline Box’s method, following
[
Box21
, §2.4]
. We consider a smooth geometrically irreducible projective curve
X/{\mathbb{Q}}
with Jacobian
J
, equipped with a degree two map
\pi:X\to C
to another smooth curve
C/{\mathbb{Q}}
. Our goal is to describe the rational points of the symmetric square
X^{(2)}
, which include pairs of conjugate quadratic points
(P,\overline{P})
of
X
.

We will use the following input:

(1)

Primes
p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}
of good reduction for
C
and
X
.

(2)

Divisors
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
generating a subgroup
G
of
J({\mathbb{Q}})
of finite index. We have an associated surjective homomorphism
\phi:{\mathbb{Z}}^{n}\to G
.

(3)

A positive integer
I
such that
I\cdot J({\mathbb{Q}})\subset G
.

(4)

A finite non-empty set
{\mathcal{L}}\subset X^{(2)}({\mathbb{Q}})
, with a fixed element
\infty\in{\mathcal{L}}
. We have a partition
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}_{pb}\coprod{\mathcal{L}}_{npb}
, with
x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{pb}
if and only if it is pulled back from a point in
C({\mathbb{Q}})
.

For each
i=1,\ldots,r
we have a subset
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\mathrm{good}}\subset{\mathcal{L}}
of known points where the (relative) Chabauty
p_{i}
-adic criterion applies. So
x\in{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\mathrm{good}}
if
[
Box21
, Theorem 2.1]
applies to
x
(which entails that
x
is the unique point of
X^{(2)}({\mathbb{Q}})
in its residue class mod
p_{i}
) or
x=\pi^{*}(P)
for
P\in C({\mathbb{Q}})
and
[
Box21
, Theorem 2.4]
applies to
x
(which entails that every point in the residue class of
x
mod
p_{i}
is pulled back from a point in
C({\mathbb{Q}})
). The following proposition is immediate from the definition.

Proposition 7.4.1
.

Suppose
x\in X^{(2)}({\mathbb{Q}})
and
\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}}(x)\in\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}}({\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\mathrm{good}})
for at least one
i=1,\ldots,r
. Then
x\in{\mathcal{L}}\cup\pi^{*}C({\mathbb{Q}})
.

In practice, we will use a version of this proposition which lends itself to explicit computation. Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{X^{(2)}({\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{red}_{p}}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{red}_{p}}
