\textstyle{X^{(2)}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{p}}
\textstyle{J({\mathbb{F}}_{p})}

where
\iota(x)=I([x]-\infty)
and
\iota_{p}
is the same map on the reduction mod
p
. For
i=1,\ldots,r
, let
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{bad}}_{i}
be the subset

{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{bad}}_{i}:=\iota_{p_{i}}^{-1}(\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}}(G))-\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}}({\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\mathrm{good}})\subset X^{(2)}({\mathbb{F}}_{p_{i}}).

It follows from Proposition
7.4.1
that if
x
is a rational point in
X^{(2)}({\mathbb{Q}})
which is not in
{\mathcal{L}}\cup\pi^{*}C({\mathbb{Q}})
, then
\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}}(x)\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{bad}}_{i}
for each
i=1\ldots r
.

Theorem 7.4.2
.

[
Box21
, Theorem 2.6]

If the set

\bigcap_{i=1}^{r}\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}}^{-1}(\iota_{p_{i}}({\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\mathrm{bad}}))\subset G

is empty, then
X^{(2)}({\mathbb{Q}})={\mathcal{L}}\cup\pi^{*}C({\mathbb{Q}})
.

Proof.

Suppose
x\in X^{(2)}({\mathbb{Q}})-({\mathcal{L}}\cup\pi^{*}C({\mathbb{Q}}))
. We have already observed that
\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}}(x)\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{bad}}_{i}
for each
i
. Then
\iota(x)\in G
and we have
\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}}(\iota(x))\in\iota_{p_{i}}({\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\mathrm{bad}})
for each
i
.
∎

For each
i
,
\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}}^{-1}(\iota_{p_{i}}({\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\mathrm{bad}}))
will give us a union of cosets of
\ker(\mathrm{red}_{p_{i}})
in
G
(possibly an empty union, if
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}^{\mathrm{bad}}=\emptyset
).

To get started applying this method in practice, we need equations for the curves and a formula for the bi-elliptic involution with quotient
X=X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
. It turns out that both curves have automorphism groups of order two, so the non-trivial automorphism must be the bi-elliptic involution.

Proposition 7.4.3
.

(1)

X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{ns}5)
is isomorphic to the plane quartic
C_{1}
with equation

9x^{4}+19x^{2}y^{2}+y^{4}+9x^{3}+19x^{2}y+22xy^{2}+2y^{3}+10x^{2}+22xy+13y^{2}+7x+12y+11=0.

It has a unique automorphism
w_{1}
of order
2
defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, given by
(x,y)\mapsto(x,-y-1)
.

(2)

X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
is isomorphic to the plane quartic
C_{2}
with equation

-x^{4}+2x^{3}y+x^{2}y^{2}+8x^{3}+2x^{2}y-2xy^{2}-y^{3}-3x^{2}-3xy+3y^{2}+2x-3y+1.

It has a unique automorphism
w_{2}
of order
2
defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, given by
(x,y)\mapsto(\frac{3x+y+2}{4x-2y+1},\frac{8x+y-8}{4x-2y+1})
.

(3)

The
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-55})
points

P_{1}=(\frac{1+\sqrt{-55}}{28},\frac{27-\sqrt{-55}}{56}),P_{2}=(\frac{3-\sqrt{-55}}{4},\frac{3+3\sqrt{-55}}{4})

of
C_{2}
have
j
-invariant
-32768
.

Proof.

We determined a model
y^{2}=-3(x^{2}+12x+144)
for the conic
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ})
in Proposition
7.2.1
. Together with our model for
X(\mathrm{ns}5)
this gives us a singular model for
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{ns}5)
. Using Magma, we determined a model for the desingularization and checked it was isomorphic to the plane quartic
C_{1}
with the given equation. The automorphisms of
C_{1}
can be computed using the function
AutomorphismGroupOfPlaneQuartic
written by Lercier, Sijsling and Ritzenthaler
[
LSR21
]
. The same procedure was used for
X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
. We also wrote down the points in the singular model (where the
j
-invariant is easy to compute) which map to
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.
∎

We write
\pi_{i}:C_{i}\to X:=X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
for the bi-elliptic quotient map.

Proposition 7.4.4
.

(1)

We have
\mathrm{rk}(\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{i}}({\mathbb{Q}}))=\mathrm{rk}(\mathrm{Jac}_{X}({\mathbb{Q}}))=1
for
i=1
and
2
.

(2)

The torsion subgroups
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{i}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}}
satisfy

•

\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}}
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}

•

\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/10{\mathbb{Z}}
(explicit generators will be identified in the proof).

(3)

Pick a generator
D\in\mathrm{Jac}_{X}({\mathbb{Q}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
. Set
G_{i}=\langle\pi_{i}^{*}D\rangle\subset\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{i}}({\mathbb{Q}})
. Then
4(\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}({\mathbb{Q}}))\subset 2G_{1}
and
10(\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{Q}}))\subset\langle 5G_{1},\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{Q}})[2]\rangle
.

Proof.

Using Chen’s isogeny (in the form of
[
dSE00
, Théoréme 2]
) and the fact that
X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ})
and
X(\mathrm{ns}5)
have genus 0, we see that
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}
is isogenous to the new part of the Jacobian of
X_{0}(225)/w_{25}
. The relevant space of cuspforms
S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(225))^{w_{25}=1,\mathrm{new}}
has dimension
3
. It has a basis of Hecke eigenforms with rational
q
-expansions, with LMFDB labels
225.2.a.b
,
225.2.a.c
and
225.2.a.e
. The associated isogeny classes of elliptic curves are those with Cremona labels
225c
,
225a
and
225d
respectively. They have Mordell–Weil rank
0
,
1
and
0
respectively, so we deduce that
\mathrm{rk}(\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}({\mathbb{Q}}))=1
.

Similarly,
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}
is isogenous to the
5
-new part of the Jacobian of
X_{0}(225)/\langle w_{9},w_{25}\rangle
. The relevant space of cuspforms is now
S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(225))^{w_{9}=1,w_{25}=1,5\mathrm{-new}}
. The newform
225.2.a.c
, with associated rank
1
elliptic curve
225a
, still contributes (as it should, since
C_{2}
maps to
X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
). We also get contributions from the
2
rational eigenforms in
S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(75))^{w_{25}=1,5\mathrm{-new}}
, labels
75.2.a.a
and
75.2.a.b
in the LMFDB (
w_{9}
has characteristic polynomial
X^{2}-1
on the oldspaces generated by each eigenform). The associated isogeny classes of elliptic curves (Cremona labels
75c
and
75a
respectively) both have rank
0
.

It now follows from
[
Box21
, Proposition 3.1]
that
2(\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{i}}({\mathbb{Q}}))\subset\langle G_{i},\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{i}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}}\rangle
for
i=1,2
. We claim that

(1)

2(\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}})=0

(2)

10(\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}})=0
.

In the first case, the orders of
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
for
p=7,11
and
13
have
\gcd

4
, so
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}}
has order dividing
4
. Moreover,
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}({\mathbb{F}}_{13})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/1710{\mathbb{Z}}
, so
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}}
must be isomorphic to a subgroup of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
.

In the second case, the orders of
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
for
p=7,11
and
13
have
\gcd

20
, so
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}}
has order dividing
20
. On the other hand, we can write down a subgroup of
\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{tors}}
isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/10{\mathbb{Z}}
, so we deduce that this is the full torsion subgroup. Generators for the torsion subgroup are given by
D_{1}
of order 10 and
D_{2}
of order 2, for

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=[(0,1,1)]-[(-3,7,1)],D_{2}

\displaystyle=5([(0,1,0)]+[(-1/2,-1/2,1)]+[P_{2}]+[\overline{P}_{2}]-2[P_{1}]-2[\overline{P}_{1}]),

where
P_{1}=(\frac{5+\sqrt{21}}{2},-\sqrt{21}-4)
,
P_{2}=(\frac{-1-\sqrt{5}}{2},\frac{3\sqrt{5}+9}{2})
and
\overline{P}_{i}
denotes the Galois conjugate of
P_{i}
.

We deduce from this that
4(\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{1}}({\mathbb{Q}}))\subset\langle 2G_{1}\rangle
and
10(\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{Q}}))\subset\langle 5G_{1},\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{Q}})[2]\rangle
.
∎

This gives us all the inputs we need to apply the relative symmetric power Chabauty method. The description of vanishing differentials and their reduction goes through exactly as in
[
Box21
, §3.4]
, replacing the Atkin–Lehner involution of
X_{0}(N)
which appears there with our bi-elliptic involutions. We used Box’s code, available at
https://github.com/joshabox/quadraticpoints/
, to carry out the computations. This also includes code written by Ozman and Siksek to search for rational points in
X^{(2)}({\mathbb{Q}})
.

Proposition 7.4.5
.

(1)

Let
X=X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{ns}5)
,
C=X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
and
\pi:X\to C
the natural quotient map. We have
X^{(2)}({\mathbb{Q}})=\pi^{*}C({\mathbb{Q}})
.

(2)

Let
X=X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
,
C=X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
and
\pi:X\to C
the natural quotient map. There are eight conjugate pairs of quadratic points of
X
which do not have image in
C({\mathbb{Q}})
. Two are defined over an imaginary quadratic field, corresponding to the points
P_{1},P_{2}
identified in Proposition
7.4.3
.

Proof.

For the first part, it turns out that we just need to apply the relative Chabauty criterion of
[
Box21
, Theorem 2.4]
for
p=43
and with
{\mathcal{L}}
consisting of 8 degree two divisors (all pulled back from
C({\mathbb{Q}})
). Digging in to what’s happening, it turns out that
\mathrm{red}_{43}(G)
is cyclic of order
7
and
\iota_{43}^{-1}(\mathrm{red}_{43}(G))
also has size
7
. The divisiors in
{\mathcal{L}}
cover all these possibilities, and they all satisfy the hypotheses of
[
Box21
, Theorem 2.4]
.

For the second part, we run Box’s Mordell–Weil sieve for the primes
11,43
,
G=\langle 5G_{1},\mathrm{Jac}_{C_{2}}({\mathbb{Q}})[2]\rangle
and
I=10
. The set
{\mathcal{L}}
includes 16 rational degree two divisors which are not pulled back from
C({\mathbb{Q}})
, 8 of which are sums of two points in
X({\mathbb{Q}})
(not interchanged by the bi-elliptic involution).
∎

This proposition has the immediate corollary:

Corollary 7.4.6
.

(1)

Let
F
be a quadratic field and
E/F
an elliptic curve such that
E
is the elliptic curve underlying an
F
-point
P\in X(\mathrm{ns}3^{\circ},\mathrm{ns}5)(F)
. Then
E
is modular.

(2)

Let
F
be an imaginary quadratic field and
E/F
an elliptic curve such that
E
is the elliptic curve underlying an
F
-point
P\in X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{ns}5)(F)
. Then
E
is modular.

Proof.

We have shown that all relevant quadratic points map to rational points of
X(\mathrm{ns}3,\mathrm{ns}5)
, with the exception of the points
P_{1},P_{2}\in X(\mathrm{s}3,\mathrm{ns}5)({\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-55}))
and their conjugates. The latter points have rational
j
-invariant so we are done in all cases.
∎

We have now completed the proof of Theorem
7.1
!
